\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\frac{{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}}{{\eta}}\right)\,.

Therefore, for any
{n}\geq{1}
, by the strong Markov property, we have:

\displaystyle\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left(\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}>{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\one\{\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}>{n}-1\}\one\{{{z}}_{{n}}\notin\bigcup_{{j}\in{\mathcal{Q}}}{\mathcal{V}}_{{j}}\}\right]

\displaystyle=\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\one\{\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}>{n}-1\}\ex_{{{z}}_{{n}-1}}\left[\one\{{{z}}_{{n}}\notin\bigcup_{{j}\in{\mathcal{Q}}}{\mathcal{V}}_{{j}}\}\right]\right]

(D.56)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\exp\left(-\frac{{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}}{{\eta}}\right)\right)\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left(\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}>{n}-1\right)\,,

where we used the
Eq.

D.55
in the last inequality.
Iterating
Eq.

D.56
, we obtain the desired result.

∎

Theorem D.3
.

Fix
{{x}}_{{0}}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
.
Under
Assumption

10
and assuming that,

(D.57)

\frac{{\mu}{R}^{2}}{1152{\overline{\sigma}^{2}_{\infty}}}>{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}-{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{x}}_{{0}}\right)\,,

for any
{\varepsilon}>0
, for
{\mathcal{V}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{V}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
neighborhoods of
{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{C}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
small enough, there is
{{\eta}}_{{0}}>0
such that for all
0<{\eta}<{\eta}_{0}
:

(D.58)

\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\tau\right]\geq e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{x}}_{{0}}\right)-{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}\,.

Remark D.1
.

Note that the condition
Eq.

D.57
is implied by the stronger condition

(D.59)

\frac{{\mu}{R}^{2}}{1152{\overline{\sigma}^{2}_{\infty}}}>{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}\,.

In particular,
{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}
does not depend on
{f}
nor on the noise distribution on the interior of the basins of attraction of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N_{\text{targ}}}
.
Indeed, by definition,
{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}
is equal to

(D.60)

{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}=\max_{{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}}\min_{{j}\in{\mathcal{Q}}}{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{{i},{j}}+1\,,

and, for
{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{j}\in{\mathcal{Q}}
, thanks to
Lemma

B.6
, we have that

(D.61)

{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{{i},{j}}=\inf\left\{{\mathcal{S}}_{{T}}({{\gamma}}):{{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{T}])\,{{\gamma}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{i}},\,{{\gamma}}_{{T}}\in{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{{j}}),\,{{\gamma}}_{n}\notin\bigcup_{{k}\in{\mathcal{V}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{{k}}\,\forall{n}\in{1,\dots,{T}-1}\right\}\,,

since for any
{x}\in{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{{j}})
, the gradient flow started at
{x}
converges to
{\mathcal{C}}_{{j}}
and has zero action cost (
Lemma

B.4
).

Hence, this condition assumption will be satisfied for functions with a sufficiently sharp profile near
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N_{\text{targ}}}
and a given profile away from it.

Proof.

Let us first begin by invoking
Lemma

D.6
:
for
{\mathcal{V}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{V}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
neighborhoods of
{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{C}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
small enough, there is
{{\eta}}_{{0}}>0
such that for all
0<{\eta}<{\eta}_{0}
,

(D.62)

e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{x}}_{{0}}\right)-{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}\leq\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\right]\,.

Also require that, for all
{j}\in{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{\mathcal{V}}_{{j}}\subset\left\{{x}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}:d\left({x},{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\right)\leq\frac{{R}}{12}\right\}
with
{R}
as in
Assumption

10
.

If
{{x}}_{{0}}\in\bigcup_{{j}\in{\mathcal{Q}}}{\mathcal{V}}_{{j}}
, then the result is trivial so let us assume that
{{x}}_{{0}}\notin\bigcup_{{j}\in{\mathcal{Q}}}{\mathcal{V}}_{{j}}
.

Let us denote by

(D.63)

{H}\coloneqq\left\{\forall{n}\geq{0},\,d({x}_{{n}+\tau},{\mathcal{Q}}_{j})\leq{R}\text{ with }{x}_{\tau}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{j}\right\}

the event that, after
SGD
hits
\bigcup_{{k}\in{\mathcal{Q}}}{\mathcal{V}}_{{k}}
at
{\mathcal{V}}_{j}
, it stays in
{\mathcal{V}}_{{j}}
forever.
Note that
Theorem

D.2
ensures that
\tau
is finite almost surely.
By the strong Markov property, we thus obtain from
Lemma

D.7
that

(D.64)

\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left({H}\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{{\mu}{R}^{2}}{1152{\overline{\sigma}^{2}_{\infty}}{\eta}}\right)\,.

Let us now start from
\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\right]
where
\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}
is the hitting time of
\bigcup_{{j}\in{\mathcal{Q}}}{\mathcal{V}}_{{j}}
by the induced chain
({{z}}_{n})_{{n}\geq{0}}
(see
Definition

8
), for which we have a lower-bound from
Lemma

D.6
.
We write

(D.65)

\displaystyle\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\one_{{H}}\right]+\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\one_{{H}^{C}}\right]\,.

We begin with the first term of the
RHS
of
Eq.

D.65
.
With the notations from
Definition

8
, let us consider
{{k}}\geq{0}
such that
\tau^{{\eta}}_{{{k}}}\lfloor{\eta}^{-1}\rfloor<\tau\leq\tau^{{\eta}}_{{{k}}+1}\lfloor{\eta}^{-1}\rfloor
(which is is possible since these hitting times are finite almost surely and
\tau>{0}
). But, on the event
{H}
,
{{z}}_{{{k}}+1}={x^{{\eta}}_{\tau^{{\eta}}_{{{k}}+1}}}={x}_{\tau^{{\eta}}_{{{k}}+1}\left\lfloor{\eta}^{-1}\right\rfloor}
must be in
{\mathcal{V}}_{j}
. This means that, on
{H}
,
\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}={{k}}+1
. Moreover,
\tau\leq{{k}}\lfloor{\eta}^{-1}\rfloor
so that, on
{H}

(D.66)

\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\leq\frac{\tau}{\lfloor{\eta}^{-1}\rfloor}+1\,.

and,

(D.67)

\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\one_{{H}}\right]\leq\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\frac{\tau}{\lfloor{\eta}^{-1}\rfloor}+1\right]\,.

Let us now focus on the second term of the
RHS
of
Eq.

D.65
.
By Fubini’s theorem for non-negative integrands, we can rewrite the expectation as:

\displaystyle\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\one_{{H}^{C}}\right]

\displaystyle=\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\int_{0}^{+\infty}\one\left\{\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}>{t}\right\}\one_{{H}^{C}}d{t}\right]

(D.68)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left(\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}>{t},{H}^{C}\right)d{t}\,.

Let us now split the integral into two parts. For
{T}>0
, we have:

\displaystyle\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\one_{{H}^{C}}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{{T}}\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left(\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}>{t},{H}^{C}\right)d{t}+\int_{{T}}^{+\infty}\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left(\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}>{t},{H}^{C}\right)d{t}

(D.69)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{{T}}\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left({H}^{C}\right)d{t}+\int_{{T}}^{+\infty}\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left(\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}>{t}\right)d{t}\,.

By
Eq.

D.64
, the first term of the
RHS
of
Eq.

D.69
is upper-bounded as:

(D.70)

\int_{0}^{{T}}\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left({H}^{C}\right)d{t}\leq{T}\exp\left(-\frac{{\mu}{R}^{2}}{1152{\overline{\sigma}^{2}_{\infty}}{\eta}}\right)\,.

By
Eq.

D.57
, take
{\varepsilon}>0
small enough so that

(D.71)

\frac{{\mu}{R}^{2}}{1152{\overline{\sigma}^{2}_{\infty}}}\geq{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}-{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{x}}_{{0}}\right)+3{\varepsilon}\,,

and define

(D.72)

{T}\coloneqq\exp\left(\frac{{\mu}{R}^{2}}{1152{\overline{\sigma}^{2}_{\infty}}{\eta}}+\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{x}}_{{0}}\right)-2{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}\right)\,.

Equation

D.70
now becomes:

(D.73)

\int_{0}^{{T}}\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left({H}^{C}\right)d{t}\leq e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{x}}_{{0}}\right)-2{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}

By
Lemma

D.8
, the second term of the
RHS
of
Eq.

D.69
can be upper-bounded as:

\displaystyle\int_{{T}}^{+\infty}\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left(\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}>{t}\right)d{t}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{T}}^{+\infty}\left(1-e^{-\frac{{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}}{{\eta}}}\right)^{{t}}d{t}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{T}}^{+\infty}\exp\left(e^{-\frac{{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}}{{\eta}}}{t}\right)d{t}

(D.74)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}}{{\eta}}}\exp\left(-\frac{{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}}{{\eta}}{T}\right)\,,

where we used
1-x\leq e^{-x}
for any
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
in the last inequality.
Injecting the definition of
{T}

Eq.

D.72
, this bound becomes

\displaystyle\int_{{T}}^{+\infty}\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left(\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}>{t}\right)d{t}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}}{{\eta}}-\exp\left(\frac{{\mu}{R}^{2}}{1152{\overline{\sigma}^{2}_{\infty}}{\eta}}+\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{x}}_{{0}}\right)-{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}-2{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}\right)\right)

(D.75)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}}{{\eta}}-\exp\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}\right)\right)\,,

where we used
Eq.

D.71
in the last inequality.
With
{\eta}>0
small enough, we finally obtain

(D.76)

\int_{{T}}^{+\infty}\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left(\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}>{t}\right)d{t}\leq 1\,.

Combining
Eq.

D.73
and
Eq.

D.76
in
Eq.

D.69
, we obtain:

(D.77)

\displaystyle\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\one_{{H}^{C}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{x}}_{{0}}\right)-2{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}+1\,,

and, combining
Eq.

D.67
and
Eq.

D.77
above in
Eq.

D.65
, we get:

(D.78)

\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\widetilde{\tau}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\right]\leq\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\frac{\tau}{\lfloor{\eta}^{-1}\rfloor}+1\right]+e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{x}}_{{0}}\right)-2{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}+1\,.

Plugging in
Eq.

D.62
yields:

(D.79)

e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{x}}_{{0}}\right)-{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}\leq\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\frac{\tau}{\lfloor{\eta}^{-1}\rfloor}+1\right]+e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{x}}_{{0}}\right)-2{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}+1\,,

which yields the desired result by taking
{\eta}>0
small enough.

∎

Let us state a variant of
Theorem

D.3
that will be useful later.

Lemma D.9
.

Fix
{i}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{N_{{\text{crit}}}}\}}
.
Under
Assumption

10
and assuming that,

(D.80)

\frac{{\mu}{R}^{2}}{1152{\overline{\sigma}^{2}_{\infty}}}>{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}-{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)\,,

for any
{\varepsilon}>0
, for
{\mathcal{V}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{V}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
neighborhoods of
{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{C}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
small enough, there is
{{\eta}}_{{0}}>0
such that for all
0<{\eta}<{\eta}_{0}
,
{x}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{{i}}
:

(D.81)

\ex_{{x}}\left[\tau\right]\geq e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)-{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}\,.

The only difference with
Theorem

D.3
is that the result is uniform over the initial state in
{\mathcal{V}}_{{i}}
instead of being specific to a given initial point.
Its proof is identical to the proof of
Theorem

D.3
: it simply uses
Lemma

D.5
to obtain the necessary variant of
Lemma

D.6
and then follows the same steps. It is therefore omitted.

D.4.
Basin-dependent convergence result

Let us recall the potential function from
[
5
]
.

Definition 15

(Potential,
[
5
, Def. 4]
)
.

Define, for
{x}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}

(D.82)

\displaystyle{{U}_{\infty}}({x})=2{{\alpha}_{\infty}}\circ{f}({x})

where
{{\alpha}_{\infty}}:{{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
is a twice continuously differentiable primitive of
1/{\sigma^{2}_{\infty}}
.

Lemma D.10
.

For any
{\gamma}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{T}])
,
{t}\in[0,{T}]
, we have

(D.83)

{{U}_{\infty}}({\gamma}({t}))-{{U}_{\infty}}({\gamma}(0))\leq{\mathcal{S}}_{0,{T}}({\gamma})\,.

Proof.

We have that, with
{{U}_{\infty}}
defined in
Definition

15
, by Young’s inequality,

\displaystyle{{U}_{\infty}}({{\gamma}}_{t})-{{U}_{\infty}}({{\gamma}}_{0})

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\langle\dot{{\gamma}}_{{s}},{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rangle}{{\sigma^{2}_{\infty}}\circ{f}({{\gamma}}_{{s}})}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\lVert\dot{{\gamma}}_{{s}}\rVert^{2}}{2{\sigma^{2}_{\infty}}\circ{f}({{\gamma}}_{{s}})}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\lVert{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rVert^{2}}{2{\sigma^{2}_{\infty}}\circ{f}({{\gamma}}_{{s}})}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\langle\dot{{\gamma}}_{{s}},{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rangle}{{\sigma^{2}_{\infty}}\circ{f}({{\gamma}}_{{s}})}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\lVert\dot{{\gamma}}_{{s}}+{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rVert^{2}}{2{\sigma^{2}_{\infty}}\circ{f}({{\gamma}}_{{s}})}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}{\mathcal{L}}({{\gamma}}_{{s}},\dot{{\gamma}}_{{s}}){\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}

(D.84)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathcal{S}}_{0,{T}}({\gamma})\,.

where we used
Lemma

B.2
in the last inequality.
∎

Lemma D.11
.

{{U}_{\infty}}
is coercive i.e.,
{{U}_{\infty}}({x})\to+\infty
as
\lVert{x}\rVert\to+\infty
.

Proof.

By
Assumption

8
,
{\sigma^{2}_{\infty}}(s)=o(s^{2})
as
s\to\infty
and therefore
{\alpha}
a primitive of
1/{\sigma^{2}_{\infty}}
must be coercive.
By coercivity of
{f}
(
Assumption

5
),
{{U}_{\infty}}=2{\alpha}\circ{f}
is also coercive.
∎

Lemma D.12
.

For any
{\mathcal{K}}\subset{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
compact, for any
{c}>0
, the set

(D.85)

\left\{{\gamma}({t}):{t}\in[0,{T}],\,{\gamma}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{T}]),{T}\in{\mathbb{N}},\,{{\gamma}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{K}},\,{\mathcal{S}}_{{t},{T}}({\gamma})\leq{c}\right\}

is included in the set

(D.86)

\left\{{x}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}:{{U}_{\infty}}({x})\leq{c}+\sup_{{\mathcal{K}}}{{U}_{\infty}}\right\}\,,

which is compact.

Proof.

Consider such a path
{\gamma}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{T}])
with
{T}\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
By
Lemma

D.10
, we have, for any
{t}\in[0,{T}]
,

(D.87)

{{U}_{\infty}}({\gamma}({t}))-{{U}_{\infty}}({\gamma}(0))\leq{\mathcal{S}}_{0,{T}}({\gamma})\leq{c}\,.

Hence the set from
Eq.

D.85
is included in

(D.88)

\left\{{x}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}:{{U}_{\infty}}({x})\leq{c}+\sup_{{\mathcal{K}}}{{U}_{\infty}}\right\}\,.

Now, by
Lemma

D.11
, the set of
Eq.

D.88
is compact, so that the set of
Eq.

D.85
is bounded.
∎

The following lemma is a slight refinement of
[
5
, Lem. D.33]
.
For any
{C}\subset\crit({f})
connected component of the set of critical points, we define its basin of attraction as the set of points from which the gradient flow converges to
{C}
:

(D.89)

{\mathrm{Attr}}({C})\coloneqq\left\{{x}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}:\lim_{{t}\to+\infty}d({\Theta}_{{t}}({x}),{C})=0\right\}\,.

Lemma D.13
.

For any
{C}\subset\crit({f})
connected component which is minimizing, for
{\delta}>0
small enough, for
{{\delta}^{\prime}}\in[0,{\delta})
small enough,

(D.90)

{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\delta}^{\prime}}({C}),{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({C}))>0\,.

Moreover,
{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({C})
can be assumed to be contained in
{\mathrm{Attr}}({C})
.

Proof.

Since
{C}
is minimizing, there exists
{\delta}_{0}>0
such that, for any
{x}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{{\delta}_{0}}({C})\setminus{C}
,
{f}({x})>{f}_{C}
where
{f}_{C}
is the value of
{f}
on
{C}
.
Moreover, by
Lemma

B.9
, taking
{\delta}>0
small enough also ensures that
{\mathcal{U}}_{{\delta}_{0}}({C})\subset{\mathrm{Attr}}({C})
.

Take
{\delta}\leq{\delta}_{0}
,
{\mathcal{U}}\coloneqq{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({C})
and
{\Delta}\coloneqq\min\left\{{{U}_{\infty}}({x})-{{\alpha}_{\infty}}({f}_{C}):{x}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}},\,d({x},{C})={\delta}/2\right\}
.
By the continuity of
{{U}_{\infty}}
and the fact that
{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
is (strictly) increasing, we have that
{\Delta}>0
.

Finally, take
{{\delta}^{\prime}}\in[0,{\delta}/2)
small enough so that, for any
{x}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{{{\delta}^{\prime}}}({C})
,
{f}({x})\leq{{\alpha}_{\infty}}({f}_{C})+{\Delta}/2
.
To conclude the proof of this lemma, we now show that
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\delta}^{\prime}}({C}),{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({C}))>0
.
Consider some
{T}>0
and
{{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{T}],{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}})
such that
{{\gamma}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{{\delta}^{\prime}}({C})
,
{{\gamma}}_{T}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({C})
.
By continuity of
{{\gamma}}
and
d(\cdot,{C})
, there exists
{t}\in[0,{T}]
such that
d({{\gamma}}_{t},{C})={\delta}/2
.
By
Lemma

D.10
, we have that

\displaystyle{\Delta}

\displaystyle\leq{{U}_{\infty}}({{\gamma}}_{t})-{{\alpha}_{\infty}}({f}_{C})

\displaystyle={{U}_{\infty}}({{\gamma}}_{t})-{{U}_{\infty}}({{\gamma}}_{0})+{{U}_{\infty}}({{\gamma}}_{0})-{{\alpha}_{\infty}}({f}_{C})

(D.91)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathcal{S}}_{0,{T}}({{\gamma}})+{\frac{{\Delta}}{2}}\,.

Since this is valid for any
{{\gamma}}
, we obtain that
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\delta}^{\prime}}({C}),{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({C}))\geq{\Delta}/2>0
.
∎

We now proceed to the key result of this section.

Lemma D.14
.

For any
{C}\subset\crit({f})
connected component which is minimizing, for any
{x}\in{\mathrm{Attr}}({C})
,

(D.92)

{\mathcal{B}}({x},{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathrm{Attr}}({C}))>0\,.

Proof.

First, let us invoke
Lemma

D.13
to obtain some
0<{{\delta}^{\prime}}<{\delta}
such that

(D.93)

{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\delta}^{\prime}}({C}),{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({C}))>0\,.

and
{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({C})\subset{\mathrm{Attr}}({C})
.

Then, by
Lemma

D.12
and continuity of
{f}
and
{\sigma^{2}_{\infty}}
, the set

(D.94)

\left\{{\sigma^{2}_{\infty}}({f}({\gamma}({t}))):{t}\in[0,{T}],\,{\gamma}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{T}]),{T}\in{\mathbb{N}},\,{{\gamma}}_{0}={x},\,{\mathcal{S}}_{{t},{T}}({\gamma})\leq 1\right\}

is bounded by some finite constant
{a}\in(0,+\infty)
.

With
({\Theta}_{t}({x}))_{{t}\geq 0}
the gradient flow starting from
{x}
(
Definition

4
), by definition of
{\mathrm{Attr}}({C})
, there exists
{S}>0
such that

(D.95)

{\Theta}_{S}({x})\in{\mathcal{U}}_{{\delta}/2}({C})\,.

Now, consider some
{T}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{T}])
such that
{{\gamma}}_{0}={x}
,
{{\gamma}}_{T}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathrm{Attr}}({C})
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{{T}}({{\gamma}})\leq 1
.

We now define

(D.96)

g_{t}\coloneqq{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert{{\gamma}}_{t}-{\Theta}_{t}({x})\rVert^{2}\,.

In particular,
{{\gamma}}
must be differentiable almost everywhere and so we have

\displaystyle\dot{g}_{t}

\displaystyle=\langle\dot{{\gamma}}_{t}-\dot{\Theta}_{t}({x}),{{\gamma}}_{t}-{\Theta}_{t}({x})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\nabla}{f}({\Theta}_{t}({x}))-{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{t}),{{\gamma}}_{t}-{\Theta}_{t}({x})\rangle+\langle\dot{{\gamma}}_{t}+{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{t}),{{\gamma}}_{t}-{\Theta}_{t}({x})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq{\beta}\lVert{{\gamma}}_{t}-{\Theta}_{t}({x})\rVert^{2}+{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert\dot{{\gamma}}_{t}+{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{t})\rVert^{2}+{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert{{\gamma}}_{t}-{\Theta}_{t}({x})\rVert^{2}

(D.97)

\displaystyle=\left({\beta}+{\frac{1}{2}}\right)g_{t}+{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert\dot{{\gamma}}_{t}+{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{t})\rVert^{2}\,,

where in the last inequality we used the smoothness of
{f}
(
Assumption

5
) and Young’s inequality.

Rewriting
Eq.

D.97
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d{t}}\left(e^{-\left({\beta}+{\frac{1}{2}}\right){t}}g_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-({\beta}+1){t}}{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert\dot{{\gamma}}_{t}+{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{t})\rVert^{2}

(D.98)

\displaystyle\leq{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert\dot{{\gamma}}_{t}+{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{t})\rVert^{2}\,,

so that integrating yields, for any
{t}\in[0,{T}]
,

(D.99)

g_{t}\leq\frac{e^{({\beta}+{\frac{1}{2}}){t}}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\dot{{\gamma}}_{{s}}+{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rVert^{2}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}

where we used that
{\Theta}_{0}({x})={x}={{\gamma}}_{0}
.

Furthermore, introducing
{a}
, we have

\displaystyle g_{t}

\displaystyle\leq{a}e^{({\beta}+{\frac{1}{2}}){t}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\lVert\dot{{\gamma}}_{{s}}+{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rVert^{2}}{{\sigma^{2}_{\infty}}({f}({{\gamma}}_{{s}}))}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}

(D.100)

\displaystyle\leq{a}e^{({\beta}+{\frac{1}{2}}){t}}{\mathcal{S}}_{0,{t}}({{\gamma}})\,,

by
Lemma

B.2
.

Hence, we have that, for any
{t}\in[0,{T}]
,

(D.101)

\lVert{{\gamma}}_{t}-{\Theta}_{t}({x})\rVert^{2}\leq 2{a}e^{({\beta}+{\frac{1}{2}}){t}}{\mathcal{S}}_{0,{t}}({{\gamma}})\,.

Therefore, if we consider
{{\gamma}}
such that, in addition,
{\mathcal{S}}_{{T}}({{\gamma}})\leq\frac{e^{-({\beta}+{\frac{1}{2}}){S}}}{2{a}}\times\frac{{{\delta}^{\prime}}^{2}}{4}
, we have that, for
{t}=\min({T},{S})
,

(D.102)

\lVert{{\gamma}}_{t}-{\Theta}_{t}({x})\rVert\leq\frac{{{\delta}^{\prime}}}{2}\,.

We now distinguish two cases:

•

If
{t}={S}
i.e.,
{S}\leq{T}
, then
Eq.

D.102
combined with
Eq.

D.95
yields that
{{\gamma}}_{S}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{{{\delta}^{\prime}}}({C})
.
But
{{\gamma}}_{T}
must be in
{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathrm{Attr}}({C})
and so in
{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{{\delta}}({C})
, so that one must have

(D.103)

{\mathcal{A}}_{T}({{\gamma}})\geq{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{U}}_{{{\delta}^{\prime}}}({C}),{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathrm{Attr}}({C}))\,.

•

If
{t}={T}
i.e.,
{T}\leq{S}
, we extend the path
{{\gamma}}
to
{\varphi}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{S}])
such that
{\varphi}_{t}={{\gamma}}_{t}
for
{t}\in[0,{T}]
and
{\varphi}_{t}={\Theta}_{t}({x})
for
{t}\in[{T},{S}]
.
In particular,
{\mathcal{S}}_{{S}}({\varphi})={\mathcal{S}}_{{T}}({{\gamma}})\leq\frac{e^{-({\beta}+{\frac{1}{2}}){T}}}{2{a}}\times\frac{{{\delta}^{\prime}}^{2}}{4}
so that the same computations leading to
Eq.

D.102
can be applied to
{\varphi}
to yield that

(D.104)

\lVert{\varphi}_{{S}}-{\Theta}_{{S}}({x})\rVert\leq\frac{{{\delta}^{\prime}}}{2}\,.

This means that
{\varphi}_{{S}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{{{\delta}^{\prime}}}({C})
which is included in
{\mathrm{Attr}}({C})
.
This is contradiction since this means that
{{\gamma}}_{T}
is in
{\mathrm{Attr}}({C})
. Therefore, this case cannot happen.

Therefore, we have shown that, for any
{{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{T}])
such that
{{\gamma}}_{0}={x}
and
{{\gamma}}_{T}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathrm{Attr}}({C})
, it holds that

(D.105)

{\mathcal{S}}_{T}({{\gamma}})\geq\min\left(1,\frac{e^{-({\beta}+{\frac{1}{2}}){T}}}{2{a}}\times\frac{{{\delta}^{\prime}}^{2}}{4},{\mathcal{B}}\left({\mathcal{U}}_{{{\delta}^{\prime}}}({C}),{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{{\delta}}({C})\right)\right)\,,

which is positive by
Eq.

D.93
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the main result of this section.

Theorem D.4
.

Consider
{{x}}_{{0}}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
that belongs to
{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{{i}})
for some
{i}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{N_{{\text{crit}}}}\}}
such that
{\mathcal{C}}_{{i}}
is minimizing.
Then, for any
{\varepsilon}>0
, for
{\mathcal{V}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{V}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
neighborhoods of
{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{C}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
small enough, there is
{{\eta}}_{{0}}>0
, an event
{H}
and a positive constant
{A}

such that for all
0<{\eta}<{\eta}_{0}
,

(D.106)

\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}({H})\geq 1-e^{-{A}/{\eta}}

and,

(D.107)

e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)-{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}\leq\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\tau^{{\eta}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\,|\,{H}\right]\leq e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)+{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}\,.

Proof.

First,
Lemma

D.14
ensures that
{\mathcal{B}}({{x}}_{{0}},{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{{i}}))>0
so that, for any
{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
{j}\neq{i}
, it holds
{\mathcal{B}}({{x}}_{{0}},{\mathcal{C}}_{{j}})>0
.
Define

(D.108)

{A}\coloneqq\min_{{j}\in{\mathcal{V}},\,{j}\neq{i}}{\mathcal{B}}({{x}}_{{0}},{\mathcal{C}}_{{j}})>0\,.

Let us now apply
Lemma

C.11
to obtain that, for any small enough neighborhoods
{{\mathcal{V}}}_{i}
of
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
,
{i}=1,\dots,{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
, there is some
{\eta}_{0}>0
such that for all
{j}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{N_{{\text{crit}}}}\}}
,
0<{\eta}<{\eta}_{0}
,

(D.109)

\probalt_{{{\mathcal{V}}}}({x},{{\mathcal{V}}}_{{j}})\leq e^{-\frac{{A}}{2{\eta}}}\,.

Now define the event
{H}
as

(D.110)

{H}\coloneqq\left\{{x^{{\eta}}_{\tau^{{\eta}}_{1}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{{i}}\right\}

with the notation of
Definition

8
.

Equation

D.109
then ensures that

(D.111)

\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}({H})\geq 1-e^{-\frac{{A}}{4{\eta}}}\,,

provided that
{\eta}
is small enough so that
e^{-\frac{{A}}{4{\eta}}}{N_{{\text{crit}}}}\leq 1
.
If
{i}\in{\mathcal{Q}}
, the result is immediate. In the following, we assume that
{i}\notin{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Let us now invoke
Lemma

D.5
to obtain that, at the potential cost of requiring smaller
{\mathcal{V}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{V}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
neighborhoods of
{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{C}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
or of reducing
{\eta}_{0}
, it holds that, for any
0<{\eta}<{\eta}_{0}
,
{x}\in{{x}}_{{0}}
,

(D.112)

e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)-{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}\leq\ex_{{x}}\left[\tau^{{\eta}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\right]\leq e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)+{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}\,.

To conclude the proof, write, by the strong Markov property,

\displaystyle\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\tau^{{\eta}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\,|\,{H}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\one_{H}\tau^{{\eta}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\right]}{\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}({H})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\one_{H}\left(1+\ex_{{x^{{\eta}}_{\tau^{{\eta}}_{1}}}}\left[\tau^{{\eta}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}\right]\right)\right]}{\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}({H})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\one_{H}\right]e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)+2{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}}{\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}({H})}

(D.113)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)+2{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}\,,

where we used
Eq.

D.112
in the last inequality and taking
{\eta}
small enough so that
e^{\frac{{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}\geq 2
.
We have thus shown the
RHS
of the inequality of the statement, and the
LHS
is obtained by the same argument.

∎

Similarly to how
Theorem

D.2
was obtained from
Theorem

D.1
, we immediately obtain the following corollary.

Theorem D.5
.

Consider
{{x}}_{{0}}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
that belongs to
{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{{i}})
for some
{i}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{N_{{\text{crit}}}}\}}
such that
{\mathcal{C}}_{{i}}
is minimizing.
Then, for any
{\varepsilon}>0
, for
{\mathcal{V}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{V}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
neighborhoods of
{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{C}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
small enough, there is
{{\eta}}_{{0}}>0
, an event
{H}
and a positive constant
{A}

such that for all
0<{\eta}<{\eta}_{0}
,

(D.114)

\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}({H})\geq 1-e^{-{A}/{\eta}}

and,

(D.115)

\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\tau\,|\,{H}\right]\leq e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)+{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}\,.

We now turn our attention to the lower-bound counterpart of
Theorem

D.5
.

Theorem D.6
.

Consider
{{x}}_{{0}}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
that belongs to
{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{{i}})
for some
{i}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{N_{{\text{crit}}}}\}}
such that
{\mathcal{C}}_{{i}}
is minimizing.
Under
Assumption

10
and assuming that,

(D.116)

\frac{{\mu}{R}^{2}}{1152{\overline{\sigma}^{2}_{\infty}}}>{{{{\mathcal{B}}}}_{\infty}}-{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)\,,

for any
{\varepsilon}>0
, for
{\mathcal{V}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{V}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
neighborhoods of
{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{C}}_{N_{{\text{crit}}}}
small enough, there is
{{\eta}}_{{0}}>0
, an event
{H}
and a positive constant
{A}

such that for all
0<{\eta}<{\eta}_{0}
,

(D.117)

\prob_{{{x}}_{{0}}}({H})\geq 1-e^{-{A}/{\eta}}

and,

(D.118)

\ex_{{{x}}_{{0}}}\left[\tau\right]\geq e^{\frac{{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)-{\varepsilon}}{{\eta}}}\,.

Theorem

D.6
is obtained by combining
Lemma

D.9
with
Lemma

D.14
in the same way as
Theorem

D.4
was obtained from
Lemma

D.5
.

D.5.
Full transition costs

Definition 16
.

We define, for
{i},{j}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{N_{{\text{crit}}}}\}}
,

\displaystyle{C}_{{i},{j}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf\left\{{\mathcal{A}}_{{N}}({\xi}):{N}\geq 1,{\xi}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{r}({N}),\,{\xi}_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{i},\,{\xi}_{{N}-1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{j}\right\}

(D.119)

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{{\mathcal{S}}_{T}({{\gamma}}):{T}\in{\mathbb{N}},{{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{r}({T}),\,{{\gamma}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{i},\,{{\gamma}}_{{T}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{j}\right\}\,.

With the same arguments as
Lemma

C.2
, we can show the alternate expression for
{C}_{{i},{j}}
.

Lemma D.15
.

For any
{i},{j}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{N_{{\text{crit}}}}\}}
, it holds that

(D.120)

{C}_{{i},{j}}=\lim_{{\delta}\to 0}{C}_{{i},{j}}^{\delta}\,,

where

\displaystyle{C}_{{i},{j}}^{\delta}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf\left\{{\mathcal{A}}_{{N}}({\xi}):{N}\geq 1,{\xi}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{r}({N}),\,{\xi}_{0}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({\mathcal{C}}_{i}),\,{\xi}_{{N}-1}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({\mathcal{C}}_{j})\right\}

(D.121)

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{{\mathcal{S}}_{T}({{\gamma}}):{T}\in{\mathbb{N}},{{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{r}({T}),\,{{\gamma}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({\mathcal{C}}_{i}),\,{{\gamma}}_{{T}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({\mathcal{C}}_{j})\right\}\,.

We can also relate
{C}_{{i},{j}}
in terms
{B}_{{i},{j}}
.

Lemma D.16
.

For any
{i}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{K}\}}
,
{j}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{N_{{\text{crit}}}}\}}
, it holds that

(D.122)

{C}_{{i},{j}}\leq{B}_{{i},{j}}\,,

and, if
{C}_{{i},{j}}<{B}_{{i},{j}}
, then there must exist
{l}\in{\mathcal{Q}}
such that
{B}_{{i},{l}}\leq{C}_{{i},{j}}<{B}_{{i},{j}}
.

This lemma is a direct consequence of the definitions of
{C}_{{i},{j}}
(
Definition

16
) and
{B}_{{i},{j}}
(
Definition

10
) and is therefore omitted.

Lemma D.17
.

For any
{i}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{N_{{\text{crit}}}}\}}
,

(D.123)

{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)\leq\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{Q}})\right\}-\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({i}\not\leadsto{\mathcal{Q}})\right\}\,.

Proof.

By
Definition

13
, we have that

(D.124)

{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)={{E}}({\mathcal{Q}})-{{E}}\left({i}\not\leadsto{\mathcal{Q}}\right)\,.

Let us begin by lower-bounding the second term.
By
Lemma

D.16
, it holds that

\displaystyle{{E}}\left({i}\not\leadsto{\mathcal{Q}}\right)

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{{{B}}}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}\left({i}\not\leadsto{\mathcal{Q}}\right)\right\}

(D.125)

\displaystyle\geq\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}\left({i}\not\leadsto{\mathcal{Q}}\right)\right\}\,.

Now, let us turn our attention to the first term: by
Lemma

D.16
, we have that

\displaystyle{{E}}({\mathcal{Q}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{{{B}}}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{Q}})\right\}

(D.126)

\displaystyle\geq\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{Q}})\right\}\,.

But, for any
{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{Q}})
which reaches that minimum, all its edges
{k}\to{l}
must be such
{{{B}}}_{{k},{l}}={C}_{{k},{l}}
by
Lemma

D.16
(otherwise we would replace it by an edge to
{\mathcal{Q}}
with a lower cost). Therefore, we must also have that

(D.127)

\displaystyle\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{Q}})\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{{{B}}}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{Q}})\right\}\,,

which shows that

(D.128)

{{E}}({\mathcal{Q}})=\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{Q}})\right\}\,.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Finally, let us restate a result from
[
5
]
that will be relevant in the following.

Lemma D.18

(
[
5
, Lem. D.31]
)
.

If
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
is not asymptotically stable, then there exists
{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}
such that
{B}_{{i},{j}}=0
and such that
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
is asymptotically stable.

D.6.
Link to topological properties of the loss landscape

The goal of this section is to prove the following result.

Proposition D.1
.

The following properties are equivalent:

(i)

For all
{x}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
,
{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{x}\right)=0
.

(ii)

For all
{i}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{K}\}}
,
{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)=0
.

(iii)

For all
{i}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{K}\}}
,
{\mathcal{K}}_{i}
is not locally minimizing.

Proof.

Items
(i)
and
(ii)
are equivalent by definition of
{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{x}\right)
(
Definition

14
).
We thus focus on the equivalence of
(ii)
and
(iii)
.

First, let us consider the case where there is some
{i}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{K}\}}
such that
{\mathcal{K}}_{i}
is locally minimizing.
By
Lemma

D.13
, there exists
{\delta}>0
small enough such that

(D.129)

{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{i},{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}_{i}))>0\,.

As a consequence, for any
{j}\neq{i}
,
{B}_{{i},{j}}>0
.

Consider
{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{V}})
such that

(D.130)

{{E}}({\mathcal{Q}})=\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{B}_{{k},{l}}\,.

Consider the graph
{g^{\prime}}
obtained by removing the edge in graph that exits
{i}
.
This edge must have positive cost and therefore

(D.131)

\displaystyle\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{B}_{{k},{l}}>\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g^{\prime}}}{B}_{{k},{l}}\geq{{E}}\left({i}\not\leadsto{\mathcal{Q}}\right)\,.

Combining
Eq.

D.130
and
Eq.

D.131
, we obtain that
{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)>0
.

Let us now turn to the case where there is no
{i}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{K}\}}
such that
{\mathcal{K}}_{i}
is locally minimizing.
Take any
{i}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{K}\}}
. We bound
{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)
with
Lemma

D.17
:

(D.132)

{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)\leq\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{Q}})\right\}-\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({i}\not\leadsto{\mathcal{Q}})\right\}\,.

Take any
{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({i}\not\leadsto{\mathcal{Q}})
. By
Lemma

D.18
, there exists
{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}
such that
{B}_{{i},{j}}=0
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{j}
is asymptotically stable. By assumption,
{j}
must thus belong to
{\mathcal{Q}}
.
Consider
{g^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{Q}})
obtained by adding the edge
{i}\to{j}
to
{g}
.

Equation

D.132
then yields that
{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{i}\right)=0
.
∎

Appendix E
Potential-based Bounds

E.1.
Noise assumptions

Assumption

11
introduces key requirements for the noise behavior through bounds on
{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}
and
{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}
around the gradient
{\nabla}{f}({x})
. Intuitively, the bound on
{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}
controls how large the noise can be (upper bound), while the bound on
{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}
ensures the noise maintains sufficient variance in all directions (lower bound) around the gradient. We then present two ways to satisfy these requirements:

Gaussian noise.

The first approach, detailed in
Lemma

E.2
, considers the case when the noise follows a truncated Gaussian distribution. This is a standard Gaussian with variance
{{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))
, but restricted to stay within a ball of radius
{R}({f}({x}))
.

Support and covariance conditions.

Lemma

E.1
provides a more general approach with two main requirements:

•

A support condition ensuring the noise can be large enough in any direction around
{\nabla}{f}({x})
with some positive probability

•

A lower bound on the covariance matrix of the noise

This second approach is more flexible as it allows for non-Gaussian noise distributions. A key example is discrete noise distributions, where
{u}({x},{\omega})
takes values in a finite set. The support condition then requires that some points in the support are sufficiently far from
{\nabla}{f}({x})
in every direction.

Assumption 11
.

For some
{X}\subset{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
, there exist
{\overline{\sigma}^{2}},{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}:{{\mathbb{R}}}\to(0,+\infty)
continuous functions such that, for all
{x}\in{X}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\forall{p}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\text{ s.t. }\lVert{p}\rVert\leq\frac{2\lVert{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert}{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))}\,,\quad

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})\leq\frac{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}}{2}

(E.1)

\displaystyle\forall{v}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\text{ s.t. }\lVert{v}-{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert\leq\lVert{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert\,,\quad

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}({x},{v})\leq\frac{\lVert{v}\rVert^{2}}{2{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))}\,.

Remark E.1
.

Assumption

11
is a generalized and detailed version of
Assumption

4
. Indeed, our blanket assumptions on the noise
Assumption

7
already imply an upper-bound on
{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})
with
{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}={\sigma^{2}_{\infty}}
for any
{x},{p}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
.
Assumption

11
allows for refined results when a tighter upper-bound on
{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})
is available for a restricted set of
{x}
and
{p}
.

Note that, in general, we will have
{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}\geq{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}
, hence the notation.

Lemma E.1
.

Assume that, for some
{X}\subset{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
relatively compact,

•

there exists
{a}>0
such that,

(E.2)

{b}\coloneqq\inf\left\{\prob\left(\left\langle{u}({x},{\omega})-{\nabla}{f}({x}),{q}\right\rangle\geq{a}+\lVert{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert\right):{x}\in{X},{q}\in{\mathbb{S}^{{{d}}-1}}\right\}>0\,,

•

there exists
{\zeta^{2}}:{{\mathbb{R}}}\to(0,+\infty)
continuous function such that, for all
{x}\in{X}
, it holds that

(E.3)

\cov{u}({x},{\omega})\succcurlyeq{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x})){{I}}\,,

Then, for any
{x}\in{X}
,
{v}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
such that
\lVert{v}-{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert\leq\lVert{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert
, it holds that

(E.4)

{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}({x},{v})\leq\max\left(1,\frac{\log{b}^{-1}}{{a}{c}}\right)\frac{\lVert{v}\rVert^{2}}{{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x}))}\,,

where
{c}>0
is a constant small enough such that

(E.5)

\forall{x}\in{X},{p}\in{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}(0,{c}),\quad\left\lVert\Hess_{{p}}{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})-\cov{u}({x},{\omega})\right\rVert\leq{\frac{1}{2}}{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x}))\,,

for the matrix norm associated with the Euclidean norm.

Note that
{c}
always exists by the relative compactness of
{X}
and the fact that

(E.6)

\Hess_{{p}}{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},0)=\cov{u}({x},{\omega})\,.

Proof.

The first part of the proof consists in showing that, for
{x}\in{X}
,
{p}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
, it holds that

(E.7)

{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})\leq\frac{{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x}))\min({c},\lVert{p}\rVert)\lVert{p}\rVert}{4}\,.

Let us first consider the case where
\lVert{p}\rVert\leq{c}
. Since both
{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},0)=0
and
{\nabla}_{p}{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},0)=\ \ex[{u}({x},{\omega})]=0
, we have that

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle{p},\Hess_{{p}}{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},t{p}){p}\right\rangle dt

\displaystyle\geq{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle{p},\cov{u}({x},{\omega}){p}\right\rangle dt-{\frac{1}{2}}{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x}))\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}\right)

(E.8)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x}))\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}}{4}\,,

where we successively used Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
Eq.

E.5
and
Eq.

E.3
.

Equation

E.8
readily yields
Eq.

E.7
when
\lVert{p}\rVert\leq{c}
.

Let us now consider the case where
\lVert{p}\rVert>{c}
. By concavity of the function
s\mapsto s^{{c}/\lVert{p}\rVert}s
on
{{\mathbb{R}}}_{+}
and Jensen’s inequality, we have that

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lVert{p}\rVert}{{c}}{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}\left({x},{c}\frac{{p}}{\lVert{p}\rVert}\right)

(E.9)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lVert{p}\rVert}{{c}}\frac{{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x}))\lVert{c}\frac{{p}}{\lVert{p}\rVert}\rVert^{2}}{4}=\frac{{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x})){c}\lVert{p}\rVert}{4}\,,

where we used
Eq.

E.8
in the last inequality. This concludes the proof of
Eq.

E.7
.

The second step of this proof now consists in showing that, for
{x}\in{X}
,
{v}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
such that
\lVert{v}-{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert\leq\lVert{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert
, it holds that

(E.10)

\argmax_{{p}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}}\left\{\left\langle{p},{v}\right\rangle-{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})\right\}=\argmin_{{p}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}}\left\{{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})-\left\langle{p},{v}\right\rangle\right\}\subset{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}\left(0,\frac{\log{b}^{-1}}{{a}}\right)\,.

For
{x}\in{X}
,
{p}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
, we can lower-bound
{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})
using
Eq.

E.2
:

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})

\displaystyle=\log\ex\left[\exp\left(\left\langle{p},{u}({x},{\omega})\right\rangle\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\left\langle{p},{\nabla}{f}({x})\right\rangle+\log\ex\left[\exp\left(\left\langle{p},{u}({x},{\omega})-{\nabla}{f}({x})\right\rangle\right)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\left\langle{p},{\nabla}{f}({x})\right\rangle+\log\ex\left[e^{\lVert{p}\rVert({a}+\lVert{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert)}\one\left\{\left\langle{u}({x},{\omega})-{\nabla}{f}({x}),\frac{{p}}{\lVert{p}\rVert}\right\rangle\geq{a}+\lVert{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert\right\}\right]

(E.11)

\displaystyle\geq\left\langle{p},{\nabla}{f}({x})\right\rangle+\lVert{p}\rVert({a}+\lVert{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert)+\log{b}\,.

As a consequence, for
{v}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
such that
\lVert{v}-{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert\leq\lVert{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert
, we obtain that

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})-\left\langle{p},{v}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\left\langle{p},{\nabla}{f}({x})-{v}\right\rangle+\lVert{p}\rVert({a}+\lVert{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert)+\log{b}

(E.12)

\displaystyle\geq\lVert{p}\rVert{a}+\log{b}\,,

where we used Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the condition on
{v}
.

In particular,
Eq.

E.12
implies that
{p}\mapsto{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})-\left\langle{p},{v}\right\rangle
is coercive on
{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
and therefore
\argmin_{{p}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}}\left\{{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})-\left\langle{p},{v}\right\rangle\right\}
is well-defined.

Remarking that
{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},0)-\left\langle 0,{v}\right\rangle=0
, we can now conclude that
\argmin_{{p}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}}\left\{{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})-\left\langle{p},{v}\right\rangle\right\}
must be included in

(E.13)

\left\{{p}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}:\lVert{p}\rVert{a}+\log{b}\leq 0\right\}={\overline{\mathbb{B}}}\left(0,\frac{\log{b}^{-1}}{{a}}\right)\,,

which completes the proof of
Eq.

E.10
.

To conclude the proof of this lemma, we now use
Eq.

E.10
to obtain that, for
{x}\in{X}
,
{v}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
such that
\lVert{v}-{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert\leq\lVert{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert
,

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}({x},{v})

\displaystyle=\sup_{{p}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}}\left\{\left\langle{p},{v}\right\rangle-{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p})\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\left\langle{p},{v}\right\rangle-{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({x},{p}):{p}\in{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}\left(0,\frac{\log{b}^{-1}}{{a}}\right)\right\}

(E.14)

\displaystyle\leq\sup\left\{\left\langle{p},{v}\right\rangle-\frac{{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x}))\min({c},\lVert{p}\rVert)\lVert{p}\rVert}{4}:{p}\in{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}\left(0,\frac{\log{b}^{-1}}{{a}}\right)\right\}\,,

where we used
Eq.

E.7
in the last inequality.

Now, we note that, for any
{p}\in{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}\left(0,\frac{\log{b}^{-1}}{{a}}\right)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x}))\min({c},\lVert{p}\rVert)\lVert{p}\rVert}{4}

\displaystyle\geq\begin{cases}\frac{{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x}))\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}}{4}&\text{if }\lVert{p}\rVert\leq{c}\\
\frac{{a}}{\log{b}^{-1}}\frac{{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x})){c}\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}}{4}&\text{if }\lVert{p}\rVert>{c}\\
\end{cases}

(E.15)

\displaystyle\geq\min\left(1,\frac{{a}{c}}{\log{b}^{-1}}\right)\frac{{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x}))\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}}{4}\,.

Plugging
Eq.

E.15
into
Eq.

E.14
, we obtain that

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}({x},{v})

\displaystyle\leq\sup\left\{\left\langle{p},{v}\right\rangle-\min\left(1,\frac{{a}{c}}{\log{b}^{-1}}\right)\frac{{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x}))\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}}{4}:{p}\in{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}\left(0,\frac{\log{b}^{-1}}{{a}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\left\{\left\langle{p},{v}\right\rangle-\min\left(1,\frac{{a}{c}}{\log{b}^{-1}}\right)\frac{{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x}))\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}}{4}:{p}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}\right\}

(E.16)

\displaystyle=\max\left(1,\frac{\log{b}^{-1}}{{a}{c}}\right)\frac{\lVert{v}\rVert^{2}}{{\zeta^{2}}({f}({x}))}\,,

which concludes the proof of this lemma.
∎

The following lemma is an immediate consequence of
Lemma

G.1
.

Lemma E.2
.

Consider
{\varepsilon}>0
.
Assume that
{u}({x},{\omega})
follows a Gaussian distribution
{\mathcal{N}}(0,{{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x})){{I}})
conditioned on being in
{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}(0,{R}({f}({x}))
for all
{x}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
and some continuous function
{{\sigma}^{2}},{R}:{{\mathbb{R}}}\to(0,+\infty)
. Assume that
8\lVert{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert\leq{R}({f}({x}))
for all
{x}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
and that
\inf_{{x}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}}\frac{{R}({f}({x}))}{{\sigma}({f}({x}))}\geq\bigoh\left(\log 1/{\varepsilon}\right)
.
Then
Assumption

11
holds with
{X}={{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
,
{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))=(1+{\varepsilon}){{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))
and
{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))=(1-{\varepsilon}){{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))
for all
{x}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
.

E.2.
Potentials and path reversal

Let us now define the potentials associated to the variance functions
{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}
and
{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}
introduced in
Assumption

11
.

Definition 17
.

Given
{\overline{\sigma}^{2}},{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}:{{\mathbb{R}}}\to(0,+\infty)
continuous functions, we define the potentials
{\overline{{U}}},{\underline{{U}}}:{{\mathbb{R}}}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}
as

(E.17)

\displaystyle{\overline{{U}}}({x})

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2{\overline{{\alpha}}}({f}({x}))\quad\text{ where ${\overline{{\alpha}}}:{{\mathbb{R}}}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}$ is a primitive of $1/{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}$}

(E.18)

\displaystyle{\underline{{U}}}({x})

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2{\underline{{\alpha}}}({f}({x}))\quad\text{ where ${\underline{{\alpha}}}:{{\mathbb{R}}}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}$ is a primitive of $1/{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}$}\,.

We begin by adapting
[
5
, Lem .E.1]
to the current setting.

Lemma E.3
.

Consider
{{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{T}])
.
Then, there exists
\widetilde{{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{S}])
a reparametrization of
{{\gamma}}
such that, for any
{t}\in[0,{S}]
,

(E.19)

\lVert\dot{\widetilde{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}\rVert=\lVert{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rVert\,.

and, under
Assumption

11
,

(E.20)

{\mathcal{S}}_{T}({{\gamma}})\geq\int_{0}^{{S}}\frac{\lVert\dot{\widetilde{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}+{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rVert^{2}}{2{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}}))}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}\,.

Proof.

By the proof
Freidlin & Wentzell [22, Chap. 4, Lem. 3.1]
, there exists
{t}({{s}})
change of time such that, with
\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}={{\gamma}}_{{t}({{s}})}
,
\lVert\dot{\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}}_{{s}}\rVert=\lVert{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}})\rVert
.

We have that

(E.21)

{\mathcal{S}}_{T}({{\gamma}})=\int_{0}^{{t}^{-1}({T})}\dot{t}({{s}}){\mathcal{L}}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}},(\dot{t}({{s}}))^{-1}\dot{\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}}_{{s}}){\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}\,,

so it suffices to bound
{\mathcal{L}}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}},\dot{\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}}_{{s}})
from below: by definition, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}},(\dot{t}({{s}}))^{-1}\dot{\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}}_{{s}})

\displaystyle\geq\sup\left\{\langle{p},(\dot{t}({{s}}))^{-1}\dot{\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}}_{{{s}}}+{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}})\rangle-{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}},{p}):\lVert{p}\rVert\leq\frac{2\lVert{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}})\rVert}{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}))}\right\}

(E.22)

\displaystyle\geq\sup\left\{\langle{p},(\dot{t}({{s}}))^{-1}\dot{\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}}_{{{s}}}+{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}})\rangle-{\frac{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}))}{2}}\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}:\lVert{p}\rVert\leq\frac{2\lVert{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}})\rVert}{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}))}\right\}\,,

by
Assumption

11
.
Applying
Lemma

G.2
with
{v}\leftarrow(\dot{t}({{s}}))^{-1}\dot{\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}}_{{t}}
,
{w}\leftarrow{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}})
and
\lambda\leftarrow(\dot{t}({{s}}))^{-1}
,
now exactly yields, for almost all
{{s}}
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}},(\dot{t}({{s}}))^{-1}\dot{\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}}_{{s}})

\displaystyle\geq{(\dot{t}({{s}}))^{-1}}\sup\left\{\langle{p},\dot{\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}}_{{{s}}}+{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}})\rangle-{\frac{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}))}{2}}\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}:\lVert{p}\rVert\leq\frac{2\lVert{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}})\rVert}{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}))}\right\}

(E.23)

\displaystyle={(\dot{t}({{s}}))^{-1}}\frac{\lVert\dot{\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}}_{{s}}+{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}})\rVert^{2}}{2{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}))}\,,

since

{p}\mapsto\langle{p},\dot{\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}}_{{{s}}}+{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}})\rangle-{\frac{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}))}{2}}\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}
reaches it maximum at
{p}^{\ast}=\frac{\dot{\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}}_{{{s}}}+{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}})}{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}))}
whose norm satisfies

\lVert{p}^{\ast}\rVert\leq\frac{2\lVert{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}})\rVert}{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}))}
.
∎

An important consequence of
Assumption

11
is that the action cost of going from
{x}
to
{{{x}^{\prime}}}
can be related to the action cost of going from
{{{x}^{\prime}}}
to
{x}
. This is formalized in
Lemma

E.4
.

Lemma E.4
.

For any
{T}>0
,
{x},{{{x}^{\prime}}}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
,
{\gamma}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{T}])
such that
{\gamma}_{0}={x}
and
{\gamma}_{T}={{{x}^{\prime}}}
, under
Assumption

11
, if
{\gamma}
is contained in
{X}
, it holds that,

(E.24)

{\mathcal{S}}_{{T}}({\gamma})\geq\inf_{{x}\in{X}}\frac{{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x})}{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))}\times\left({\mathcal{B}}({x},{{{x}^{\prime}}})+{\overline{{U}}}({{{x}^{\prime}}})-{\overline{{U}}}({x})\right)\,,

Proof.

Let us first invoke
Lemma

E.3
: there exists
\widetilde{{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{S}])
a reparametrization of
{{\gamma}}
such that, for any
{t}\in[0,{S}]
,

(E.25)

\lVert\dot{\widetilde{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}\rVert=\lVert{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rVert\,.

and,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{T}({{\gamma}})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{{S}}\frac{\lVert\dot{\widetilde{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}+{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rVert^{2}}{2{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}}))}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}

(E.26)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{{x}\in{X}}\frac{{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x})}{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))}\times\left(\int_{0}^{{S}}\frac{\lVert\dot{\widetilde{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}+{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rVert^{2}}{2{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}}))}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}\right)\,.

It now suffices to lower-bound the integral on the
RHS
of
Eq.

E.26
.
We have

(E.27)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{{S}}\frac{\lVert\dot{\widetilde{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}+{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rVert^{2}}{2{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}}))}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{{S}}\frac{\lVert-\dot{\widetilde{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}+{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rVert^{2}}{2{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}}))}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}+\int_{0}^{{S}}\frac{2\langle\dot{\widetilde{{\gamma}}}_{{s}},{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rangle}{{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}}))}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}\,.

By definition of
{\overline{{U}}}
(
Definition

17
) the second integral is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{{S}}\frac{2\langle\dot{\widetilde{{\gamma}}}_{{s}},{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rangle}{{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}}))}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{{S}}\langle\dot{\widetilde{{\gamma}}}_{{s}},{\nabla}{\overline{{U}}}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rangle{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}

(E.28)

\displaystyle={\overline{{U}}}({{{x}^{\prime}}})-{\overline{{U}}}({x})\,.

For the first integral in
Eq.

E.27
, define the path
({\varphi}_{{s}})_{{{s}}\in[0,{S}]}
by
{\varphi}_{{s}}=\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{S}-{{s}}}
. We have that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{{S}}\frac{\lVert-\dot{\widetilde{{\gamma}}}_{{s}}+{\nabla}{f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}})\rVert^{2}}{2{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}(\widetilde{{\gamma}}_{{s}}))}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{{S}}\frac{\lVert\dot{{\varphi}}_{{s}}+{\nabla}{f}({\varphi}_{{s}})\rVert^{2}}{2{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}{f}({\varphi}_{{s}}))}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{{S}}{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}({\varphi}_{{s}},\dot{\varphi}_{{s}}+{\nabla}{f}({\varphi}_{{s}})){\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{{s}}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{S}}_{{S}}({\varphi})

(E.29)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathcal{B}}({x},{{{x}^{\prime}}})\,,

where we used
Assumption

11
in the first inequality with
{v}\leftarrow\dot{\varphi}_{{s}}+{\nabla}{f}({\varphi}_{{s}})
whose norm satisfies the condition of
Assumption

11
by
Eq.

E.25
.
Plugging
Eq.

E.29
and
Eq.

E.29
into
Eq.

E.27
and then into
Eq.

E.26
yields the desired result.
∎

E.3.
Graph representation

Let us reuse the notation of
Appendix

D
.
In
Appendix

D
, we consider graphs over the set of vertices
{\mathcal{V}}={\{1,\dotsc,{N_{{\text{crit}}}}\}}
and the set of edges
({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}})\times{\mathcal{V}}
where
{\mathcal{Q}}=\left\{1,\dots,{N_{\text{targ}}}\right\}
.

Let us consider a set of particular edges
{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}}\subset({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}})\times{\mathcal{V}}
such that

(E.30)

{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}}\coloneqq\left\{{i}\to{j}\in({\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}})\times{\mathcal{V}}:{i}\neq{j},\,\cl{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{i})\cap\cl{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{j})\neq\emptyset\right\}\,.

Note that by
Lemma

B.8
, we have that
{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{i})
,
{i}\in{\{1,\dotsc,{N_{{\text{crit}}}}\}}
is a partition of
{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
. Therefore,
({\mathcal{V}},{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}})
still satisfies that, from every
{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}
there is a path that leads to
{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Lemma E.5
.

For any
{i}\to{j}\in{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}}
, let us define

(E.31)

{x}_{{i},{j}}\in\argmin_{{x}\in\cl{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{i})\cap\cl{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{j})}{f}({x})\,.

Then, under
Assumption

11
, if
{X}
is large enough so that it contains the bounded set

(E.32)

\left\{{x}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}:{f}({x})\leq\max_{{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}},{j}\in{\mathcal{V}},{i}\neq{j}}{f}({x}_{{i},{j}})+1\right\}\,,

for any
{i}\to{j}\in{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}}
, it holds that

(E.33)

{C}_{{i},{j}}\leq{\overline{{U}}}({x}_{{i},{j}})-{\overline{{U}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{i})\,.

Proof.

Note that the coercivity of
{f}
(
Assumption

5
) ensure that
{x}_{{i},{j}}
is well-defined and the set of
Eq.

E.32
is bounded.

Take
{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
{i}\neq{j}
. We first consider the case where
{\nabla}{f}({x}_{{i},{j}})\neq 0
.
Fix
{\varepsilon}>0
.

By
Lemma

D.15
, we have that there exists
{\delta}_{0}>0
such that, for any
{\delta}\in(0,{\delta}_{0})
,

(E.34)

{C}_{{i},{j}}\leq{C}_{{i},{j}}^{\delta}+{\varepsilon}\,.

At the potential cost of reducing
{\delta}_{0}>0
, assume that, for any
{x}\in{\mathbb{B}}({x}_{{i},{j}},{\delta}_{0}/2)
, it holds that

(E.35)

{a}\leq\lVert{\nabla}{f}({x})\rVert\leq{b}\quad\text{ and }\quad{\overline{\sigma}^{2}_{\infty}}({f}({x}))\geq{c}\,,

for some
0<{a}\leq{b}
and
{c}>0
.
Moreover, by continuity of
{f}
and
{\overline{{U}}}
, also assume that
{\delta}_{0}
is small enough so that, for any
{x}\in{\mathbb{B}}({x}_{{i},{j}},{\delta}_{0}/2)
, it holds that

(E.36)

{\overline{{U}}}({x})\leq{\overline{{U}}}({x}_{{i},{j}})+{\varepsilon}

and,

(E.37)

{f}({x})\leq{f}({x}_{{i},{j}})+1\,.

Finally, also assume that
{\delta}_{0}
is small enough such that, for any
{x}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({\mathcal{C}}_{i})
, it holds that

(E.38)

{\overline{{U}}}({x})\geq{\overline{{U}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{i})-{\varepsilon}\,.

Fix
{\delta}\in(0,{\delta}_{0})
such that
{\delta}\leq{\varepsilon}
.
Now, since
{x}_{{i},{j}}\in\cl{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{i})\cap\cl{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{j})
, there exist
{x}_{i}\in{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{i})\cap{\mathbb{B}}({x}_{{i},{j}},{\delta}/2)
and
{x}_{j}\in{\mathrm{Attr}}({\mathcal{C}}_{j})\cap{\mathbb{B}}({x}_{{i},{j}},{\delta}/2)
.
By construction, the gradient flows started at
{x}_{i}
and
{x}_{j}
converge respectively to
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
.
Hence, there exists
{T}\geq 1
integer such that
{\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2}({x}_{i})\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({\mathcal{C}}_{i})
and
{\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2}({x}_{j})\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({\mathcal{C}}_{j})
for all
\alpha\coloneqq{\delta}/{a}
.

Let us now estimates the action costs of the paths
({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-{t}}({x}_{i}))_{{t}\in[0,{T}-\alpha/2]}
and
({\Theta}_{{t}}({x}_{j}))_{{t}\in[0,{T}-\alpha/2]}
.
By
Eq.

E.37
and definition of the flow, the whole path
({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-{t}}({x}_{i}))_{{t}\in[0,{T}-\alpha/2]}
is contained in
Eq.

E.32
and therefore in
{X}
.
Therefore, by
Assumption

11
, we have that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{{T}-\alpha/2}({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-\cdot}({x}_{i}))

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{{T}-\alpha/2}{\mathcal{L}}({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-{t}}({x}_{i}),{\nabla}{f}({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-{t}}({x}_{i}))){\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{{T}-\alpha/2}{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-{t}}({x}_{i}),2{\nabla}{f}({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-{t}}({x}_{i}))){\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{t}

(E.39)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{{T}-\alpha/2}\frac{4\lVert{\nabla}{f}({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-{t}}({x}_{i}))\rVert^{2}}{2{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-{t}}({x}_{i})))}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{t}\,,

where we used
Assumption

11
in the last inequality.
Rewriting this integral we obtain,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{{T}-\alpha/2}({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-\cdot}({x}_{i}))

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{{T}-\alpha/2}\frac{2\langle{\nabla}{f}({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-{t}}({x}_{i})),\dot{\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-{t}}({x}_{i})\rangle}{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-{t}}({x}_{i})))}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{{T}-\alpha/2}2\langle{\nabla}{\overline{{U}}}({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-{t}}({x}_{i})),\dot{\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-{t}}({x}_{i})\rangle{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{t}

\displaystyle={\overline{{U}}}({x}_{{i}})-{\overline{{U}}}({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2}({x}_{i}))

(E.40)

\displaystyle\leq{\overline{{U}}}({x}_{{i},{j}})-{\overline{{U}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{i})+2{\varepsilon}\,,

by
Eqs.

E.36
and
E.38
.

For the path
({\Theta}_{{t}}({x}_{j}))_{{t}\in[0,{T}-\alpha/2]}
, since it is a trajectory of the gradient flow, we have that

(E.41)

{\mathcal{S}}_{{T}-\alpha/2}({\Theta}_{\cdot}({x}_{j}))=0\,.

Finally, let us consider the path
({{\gamma}}_{t})_{{t}\in[0,\alpha]}
defined by
{{\gamma}}_{t}={x}_{i}+\alpha^{-1}{t}({x}_{j}-{x}_{i})
.
We have
\dot{{\gamma}}_{t}=\alpha^{-1}({x}_{j}-{x}_{i})
so that
\lVert\dot{{\gamma}}_{t}\rVert\leq{\delta}/\alpha={a}\leq\lVert{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{t})\rVert
by
Eq.

E.35
.
Moreover,
Eq.

E.37
ensures that
{{\gamma}}_{t}\in{X}
for all
{t}\in[0,\alpha]
.

Therefore, by
Assumption

11
, we have that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}({{\gamma}})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\alpha}{\mathcal{L}}({{\gamma}}_{t},\dot{{\gamma}}_{t}){\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\alpha}{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}({{\gamma}}_{t},{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{t})\dot{{\gamma}}_{t}){\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\alpha}\frac{\lVert\dot{{\gamma}}_{t}+{\nabla}{f}({{\gamma}}_{t})\rVert^{2}}{2{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({{\gamma}}_{t}))}{\kern 0.0pt\>d\mkern-1.0mu{}}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\frac{({a}+{b})^{2}}{2{c}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\delta}({a}+{b})^{2}}{2{a}{c}}

(E.42)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{{\varepsilon}({a}+{b})^{2}}{2{a}{c}}\,.

Putting the three paths
({\Theta}_{{T}-\alpha/2-\cdot}({x}_{i}))_{{t}\in[0,{T}-\alpha/2]}
,
({{\gamma}}_{t})_{{t}\in[0,\alpha]}
and
({\Theta}_{{t}}({x}_{j}))_{{t}\in[0,{T}-\alpha/2]}
together, we obtain a path that goes from
{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({\mathcal{C}}_{i})
to
{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}({\mathcal{C}}_{j})
in integer time
2{T}
and whose action cost is upper-bounded by

(E.43)

{\overline{{U}}}({x}_{{i},{j}})-{\overline{{U}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{i})+2{\varepsilon}+\frac{{\varepsilon}({a}+{b})^{2}}{2{a}{c}}\,.

Combined with
Eq.

E.34
, this yields

(E.44)

{C}_{{i},{j}}\leq{\overline{{U}}}({x}_{{i},{j}})-{\overline{{U}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{i})+3{\varepsilon}+\frac{{\varepsilon}({a}+{b})^{2}}{2{a}{c}}\,,

which yields the result.

Let us now briefly discuss the case where
{\nabla}{f}({x}_{{i},{j}})=0
.
In this case,
{x}_{{i},{j}}
belongs to some
{\mathcal{C}}_{{l}}
,
{l}\in{\mathcal{V}}
.
To adapt the proof above, it suffices to require that
{x}_{i}
and
{x}_{j}
are close enough to
{x}_{{i},{j}}
so that they belong to
{\mathcal{W}}_{\varepsilon}({\mathcal{C}}_{{l}})
(give by
Lemma

B.6
) and to use
Lemma

B.7
to build a path from
{x}_{i}
to
{x}_{j}
with cost at most
{\varepsilon}
.
∎

We provide an iterated version of
Lemma

E.5
that will be convenient in the following.

Corollary E.1
.

For any
{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
define

(E.45)

{\overline{{C}}}_{{i},{j}}\coloneqq\min\left\{\sum_{{n}=0}^{{N}-1}{\overline{{U}}}({x}_{{j}_{{n}},{j}_{{n}+1}})-{\overline{{U}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{{j}_{{n}}}):{j}_{0}={i},\,{j}_{{N}}={j},\,{j}_{{n}}\to{j}_{{n}+1}\in{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}}\right\}\,.

Then, under the same assumptions as
Lemma

E.5
, it holds that

(E.46)

{C}_{{i},{j}}\leq{\overline{{C}}}_{{i},{j}}\,.

Let us now specify how big enough we will need the set
{X}
from
Assumption

11
to be.

Lemma E.6
.

Define

(E.47)

{C_{\infty}}\coloneqq\max_{{i},{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}}{C}_{{i},{j}}+1<+\infty\,.

Then, if
{X}
is large enough so that it contains the bounded set

(E.48)

\left\{{x}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}:{{U}_{\infty}}({x})\leq\sup_{{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}},{x}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{i}}{\overline{{U}}}({x})+{C_{\infty}}\right\}\,,

then, for any
{i},{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
, it holds that
{C}_{{i},{j}}
is equal to

(E.49)

\inf\left\{{\mathcal{S}}_{T}({\gamma}):{\gamma}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{T}]),\,{T}\in{\mathbb{N}},\,{\gamma}_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{i},\,{\gamma}_{T}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{j},\,\left\{{\gamma}({t}):{t}\in[0,{T}]\right\}\subset{X}\right\}\,.

Proof.

Note that
{C_{\infty}}
is finite by
Assumption

9
.
The rest of the result is then a direct consequence of
Lemma

D.12
.
∎

We can now combine
Lemma

E.4
with
Lemma

E.6
to obtain the following result.

Lemma E.7
.

Under
Assumption

11
, if
{X}
is large enough so that it satisfies the requirement of
Lemma

E.6
, then for any
{i},{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{i}\neq{j}
, it holds that

(E.50)

{C}_{{j},{i}}-{C}_{{i},{j}}\leq{\Delta}{C}_{{j},{i}}+(1-{\Delta})({\overline{{U}}}_{{i}}-{\overline{{U}}}_{{j}})\,,

where
{\Delta}\in[0,1]
satisfies

(E.51)

{\Delta}\geq\sup_{{x}\in{X}}1-\frac{{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))}{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))}\,.

Proof.

Lemma

E.6
allows us to consider only paths that are contained in
{X}
to compute
{C}_{{i},{j}}
.
Taking the infimum over such paths in
Lemma

E.4
yields:

(E.52)

{C}_{{i},{j}}\geq(1-{\Delta})({C}_{{j},{i}}+({\overline{{U}}}_{{j}}-{\overline{{U}}}_{{i}}))\,.

Rearranging the terms, we obtain the result.
∎

To simplify the notation for the following lemma, let us define, for
{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
{i}\neq{j}
,

(E.53)

{\overline{{U}}}_{{i},{j}}\coloneqq{\overline{{U}}}({x}_{{i},{j}})\quad\text{ and }\quad{\overline{{U}}}_{{j}}\coloneqq{\overline{{U}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{j})\,.

Lemma E.8
.

Assume that
Assumption

11
holds with
{X}
satisfying the assumptions of
Lemmas

E.5
and
E.6
.

For any
{{i}}_{{0}}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({{i}}_{{0}}\nrightarrow{\mathcal{Q}})
, there exists
{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
such that there is a path from
{{i}}_{{0}}
to
{i}
in
{g}
and there is
{g^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that the cost difference between
{g}
and
{g^{\prime}}

(E.54)

\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g^{\prime}}}{C}_{{k},{l}}-\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}

is upper-bounded by

\displaystyle\min\bigg\{\max_{l=0,\dots,p-1}

\displaystyle\max\left({\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{l},{j}_{l+1}}-{\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{l}},{\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{l},{j}_{l+1}}-{\overline{{U}}}_{{i}}\right)+2{\Delta}({N_{{\text{crit}}}}^{2}+1)\sup_{{X}}{\overline{{U}}}:

(E.55)

\displaystyle{j}_{0}={i},\,{j}_{p}\in{\mathcal{Q}},\,{j}_{l}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}},\,{j}_{l}\to{j}_{l+1}\in{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}},\,l=0,\dots,p-1\bigg\}\,,

where
{\Delta}\in[0,1]
satisfies

(E.56)

{\Delta}\geq\sup_{{x}\in{X}}1-\frac{{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))}{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))}\,.

Proof.

By definition of
{\mathcal{G}}({{i}}_{{0}}\nrightarrow{\mathcal{Q}})
(
Section

D.1
), there exists
{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
{i}\neq{j}
such that if we were to add the edge
{i}\to{j}
to
{g}
, it would be a
{\mathcal{Q}}
-graph.
In other words,
{i}\to{j}
is not in
{g}
and there are paths from
{{i}}_{{0}}\to{i}
and
{j}\to{\mathcal{Q}}
in
{g}
.

Now, take consider a path that goes from
{i}
to
{j}
with only edges from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}}
:
{j}_{0}={i}
,
{j}_{p}={j}
and
{j}_{l}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{j}_{l}\to{j}_{l+1}\in{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}}
,
l=0,\dots,p-1
.

Define
k\geq 0
to be the smallest integer such that there is a path from
{j}_{k}
to
{\mathcal{Q}}
in
{g}
.
Such a
k
exists since there is a path from
{j}
to
{\mathcal{Q}}
in
{g}
, and
k\geq 1
since there is no path from
{i}
to
{\mathcal{Q}}
in
{g}
.
In particular, there is no path from
{j}_{k-1}
to
{\mathcal{Q}}
in
{g}
. From the definition of the graphs
{\mathcal{G}}({{i}}_{{0}}\nrightarrow{\mathcal{Q}})
, this implies either there is a path from
{i}
to
{j}_{k-1}
in
{g}
or a path from
{j}_{k-1}
to
{i}
in
{g}
.

•

If there is a path from
{i}
to
{j}_{k-1}
in
{g}
, then we consider the graph
{g^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{Q}})
obtained by adding the edge
{j}_{k-1}\to{j}_{k}
to
{g}
.
Since
{j}_{k-1}\to{j}_{k}
is in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}}
, by
Lemma

E.5
, the cost of adding that edge is bounded as

(E.57)

{C}_{{j}_{k-1},{j}_{k}}\leq{\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{k-1},{j}_{k}}-{\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{k-1}}\,,

in which case
Eq.

E.55
is immediately satisfied.

•

If there is a path from
{j}_{k-1}
to
{i}
in
{g}
, then we consider the graph
{g^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{Q}})
obtained by reversing the path from
{j}_{k-1}
to
{i}
in
{g}
and adding the edge
{j}_{k-1}\to{j}_{k}
.
Let us denote by
{m}_{0}={j}_{k-1}
,
{m}_{q}={i}
and
{m}_{l}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{m}_{l}\to{m}_{l+1}\in{g}
,
l=0,\dots,q-1
the path from
{j}_{k-1}
to
{i}
.
By
Lemmas

E.7
and
E.5
the cost of reversing that path and adding the edge
{j}_{k-1}\to{j}_{k}
is upper-bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{q-1}\left({C}_{{m}_{l+1},{m}_{l}}-\sum_{l=0}^{p-1}{C}_{{m}_{l},{m}_{l+1}}\right)+{C}_{{j}_{k-1},{j}_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=0}^{q-1}\left({\Delta}{C}_{{m}_{l+1},{m}_{l}}+(1-{\Delta})({\overline{{U}}}_{{m}_{l}}-{\overline{{U}}}_{{m}_{l+1})}\right)+{\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{k-1},{j}_{k}}-{\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{k-1}}

(E.58)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{q-1}{{\Delta}{C}_{{m}_{l+1},{m}_{l}}+(1-{\Delta})({\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{k-1}}-{\overline{{U}}}_{{i}})}+{\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{k-1},{j}_{k}}-{\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{k-1}}\,.

Now,
Corollary

E.1
ensures that the sum
\sum_{l=0}^{q-1}{{\Delta}{C}_{{m}_{l+1},{m}_{l}}}
is upper-bounded by
2{N_{{\text{crit}}}}^{2}{\Delta}\sup_{{X}}{\overline{{U}}}
so we obtain that the
RHS
of
Eq.

E.58
is upper-bounded by

(E.59)

\displaystyle 2{N_{{\text{crit}}}}^{2}{\Delta}\sup_{{X}}{\overline{{U}}}+{\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{k-1},{j}_{k}}-{\overline{{U}}}_{{i}}+2{\Delta}\sup_{{X}}{\overline{{U}}}\,,

which concludes the proof.

∎

To obtain the main result, we will require the following lemma.

Lemma E.9
.

Under
Assumption

11
, if
{X}
is large enough so that it satisfies the assumptions of
Lemma

E.6
, then for any
{i},{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{i}\neq{j}
, it holds that
For any
{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
{i}\neq{j}
, it holds that

(E.60)

{C}_{{i},{j}}\geq{\underline{{C}}}_{{i},{j}}\coloneqq\inf\left\{\sup_{{{s}}<{t}}\left({\underline{{U}}}({{\gamma}}_{{t}})-{\underline{{U}}}({{\gamma}}_{{{s}}})\right):{{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,{T}]),\,{T}\in{\mathbb{N}},\,{{\gamma}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{i},\,{{\gamma}}_{T}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{j}\right\}\,.

Proof.

This lemma follows the same proof as
Lemma

D.10
with the additional use of
Lemma

E.6
to be able to leverage
Assumption

11
.
∎

We are now finally ready to prove the main result.

Theorem E.1
.

Assume that
Assumption

11
holds with
{X}
satisfying the assumptions of
Lemmas

E.5
and
E.6
.

For any
{{i}}_{{0}}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
,
define

(E.61)

r\coloneqq\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{\overline{{C}}}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({{i}}_{{0}}\not\leadsto{\mathcal{Q}})\right\}\,.

there exists
{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
such that
{\underline{{C}}}_{{{i}}_{{0}},{i}}\leq r
such that
{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{i}}_{{0}}\right)
is upper-bounded by

\displaystyle\min\bigg\{\max_{l=0,\dots,p-1}

\displaystyle\max\left({\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{l},{j}_{l+1}}-{\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{l}},{\overline{{U}}}_{{j}_{l},{j}_{l+1}}-{\overline{{U}}}_{{i}}\right)+2{\Delta}({N_{{\text{crit}}}}^{2}+1)\sup_{{X}}{\overline{{U}}}:

(E.62)

\displaystyle{j}_{0}={i},\,{j}_{p}\in{\mathcal{Q}},\,{j}_{l}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}},\,{j}_{l}\to{j}_{l+1}\in{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}},\,l=0,\dots,p-1\bigg\}\,,

where
{\Delta}\in[0,1]
satisfies

(E.63)

{\Delta}\geq\sup_{{x}\in{X}}1-\frac{{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))}{{\overline{\sigma}^{2}}({f}({x}))}\,.

Proof.

By
Lemma

D.16
,

{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{i}}_{{0}}\right)
is upper-bounded as

(E.64)

{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{i}}_{{0}}\right)\leq\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{Q}})\right\}-\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({{i}}_{{0}}\not\leadsto{\mathcal{Q}})\right\}\,.

By
Corollary

E.1
, we have that

(E.65)

\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({{i}}_{{0}}\not\leadsto{\mathcal{Q}})\right\}\leq\min\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{\overline{{C}}}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({{i}}_{{0}}\not\leadsto{\mathcal{Q}})\right\}=r\,.

Hence, take
{g}\in\argmin\left\{\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}:{g}\in{\mathcal{G}}({\mathcal{Q}}\nrightarrow{\mathcal{Q}})\right\}
and it must satisfy

(E.66)

\sum_{{k}\to{l}\in{g}}{C}_{{k},{l}}\leq r\,.

Apply
Lemma

E.8
to obtain that there exists
{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
such that there is a path from
{{i}}_{{0}}
to
{i}
in
{g}
and there is
{g^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that the cost difference between
{g}
and
{g^{\prime}}
is bounded by
Eq.

E.62
.
Combined with
Eq.

E.64
, this yields that
{{E}}\left({\mathcal{Q}}\,|\,{{i}}_{{0}}\right)
is upper-bounded by
Eq.

E.62
.

Finally, denote by
{j}_{0}={{i}}_{{0}}
,
{j}_{p}={i}
and
{j}_{l}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{j}_{l}\to{j}_{l+1}\in{g}
,
l=0,\dots,p-1
the path from
{{i}}_{{0}}
to
{i}
in
{g}
.

Equation

E.66
ensures that

(E.67)

r\geq\sum_{l=0}^{p-1}{C}_{{j}_{l},{j}_{l+1}}\geq{\underline{{C}}}_{{{i}}_{{0}},{i}}\,,

which, combined with
Lemma

E.9
, implies that
{\underline{{C}}}_{{{i}}_{{0}},{i}}\leq r
.
∎

Appendix F
Numerical validation

In this last appendix, we provide some more numerical experiments as validation of the estimates provided by
Theorems

1
and
2
.
For concreteness, we consider the following multimodal test functions:

(1)

The Styblinski–Tang function:

(F.1a)

{f}({x}_{1},{x}_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{i}=1}^{{d}}({x}_{{i}}^{4}-16{x}_{{i}}^{2}+5{x}_{{i}})

(2)

The Himmelblau function:

(F.1b)

{f}({x}_{1},{x}_{2})=({x}_{1}^{2}+{x}_{2}-11)^{2}+({x}_{1}+{x}_{2}^{2}-7)^{2}

For the Styblinski–Tang function, we treat the
2
-dimensional case in order to facilitate comparisons with the other examples, and for ease of visualization.

In
Fig.

4
, we plot the landscape of
{f}
for each of the above cases, as well as the runtime statistics of (
SGD
) in each case.
As in
Fig.

2
, we considered different values of the algorithm’s learning rate
{\eta}
, and for each value of
{\eta}
, we performed
500
runs of (
SGD
) with Gaussian noise (
{\sigma}=50
).
Then, for each “particle” of (
SGD
), we recorded the number of iterations required to reach the vicinity of
{p}_{1}
(within
{\delta}=10^{-2}
) from the same, fixed initialization.
The resulting boxplots are displayed in
Fig.

4
in log-inverse scale, with a point density overlay to illustrate the full empirical distribution of
{\tau}
for every
{\eta}
.

(a)

Loss landscape and hitting time statistics for the Styblinski–Tang function (
F.1a
).

(b)

Loss landscape and hitting time statistics for the Himmelblau function (
F.1b
).

Figure 4
.
Visualization and hitting time statistics for the Styblinski–Tang (top) and Himmelblau functions (bottom).
In both cases, the loss landscape is superimposed on the stable and unstable manifolds of the function’s critical points, which define the edges of its transition graph (the arrows indicate the direction of the gradient flow, and hence the transitions which come at no cost).

In both cases, we plotted the theoretical prediction of
Theorem

1
(labeled ‘‘Theory’’), against a linear regression plot of the observed runtime data (labeled ‘‘Data’’).
13
13

13

In more detail, we plotted a linear fit with slope given by
Theorem

1
.

For the Styblinski–Tang function, we observe a situation similar to the three-hump-camel example of
Fig.

2
:
Both theory and experiment indicate that the global convergence time
{\tau}
of (
SGD
) scales exponentially in the inverse of the algorithm’s step-size
{\eta}
, and there is excellent agreement between theory and experiment (
R^{2}=0.973
and
R^{2}=0.989
respectively).
By contrast, in the Himmelblau case, runtimes are significantly slower, and the slope of the theoretical estimator is
0
:
This is because the Himmelblau function does not have any spurious minima, so
{\tau}
enters the subexponential regime;
this is confirmed by the fact that the algorithm’s runtime is several orders of magnitude smaller, with strong agreeemnt between our theoretical estimate and the numerically measured values (
R^{2}=0.913
and
R^{2}=0.976
respectively).

Appendix G
Auxiliary results

G.1.
Truncated Gaussian distribution

Let us restate two technical lemmas from
[
5
]
on which we rely.
The first one provides bounds on the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian of a truncated Gaussian distribution.

Lemma G.1

(
[
5
, Lem. F.2]
)
.

Consider
{X}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,{{\sigma}^{2}}{{I}})
a multivariate Gaussian distribution with
{{\sigma}^{2}}>0
. For
{R}>0
, define the truncated Gaussian
rv
(
rv
)
{X_{R}}
by conditioning
{X}
to the ball
{\overline{\mathbb{B}}}(0,{R})
.
Define

(G.1)

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({p})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\log\ex\left[e^{\langle{p},{X_{R}}\rangle}\right]

(G.2)

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}({p})

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}^{*}({p})\,,

and

(G.3)

{E}({{\sigma}^{2}},{R})\coloneqq e^{-\tfrac{{R}^{2}}{16{{\sigma}^{2}}}}2^{{{d}}+3}({{d}}+1)

and assume that
{R}>0
satisfies

(G.4)

{R}\geq 4{\sigma}\sqrt{({{d}}+3)\log 2+\log({{d}}+1)}\,.

Then, for any
{p}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
such that
\lVert{p}\rVert\leq\frac{{R}}{2{{\sigma}^{2}}}
,
{v}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
such that
\lVert{v}\rVert\leq\frac{{R}}{4}
, it holds that

(G.5)

\displaystyle\left(1-{E}({{\sigma}^{2}},{R})\right)\frac{{{\sigma}^{2}}\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}}{2}\leq

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathcal{H}}}({p})\leq\left(1+{E}({{\sigma}^{2}},{R})\right)\frac{{{\sigma}^{2}}\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}}{2}

(G.6)

\displaystyle\left(1-2{E}({{\sigma}^{2}},{R})\right)\frac{\lVert{v}\rVert^{2}}{2{{\sigma}^{2}}}\leq

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathcal{L}}}({v})\leq\left(1+2{E}({{\sigma}^{2}},{R})\right)\frac{\lVert{v}\rVert^{2}}{2{{\sigma}^{2}}}\,.

We will also require the following technical lemma.

Lemma G.2

(
[
5
, Lem. F.3]
)
.

Consider
{v},{w}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}
such that
0<\lVert{w}\rVert\leq\tfrac{\mu{R}}{2}
for some
{R},\mu>0
.
Define,

(G.7)

{f}(u)=\sup_{{p}\in{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{{{d}}}}:\lVert{p}\rVert\leq{R}}\langle{p},u\rangle-{\frac{\mu}{2}}\lVert{p}\rVert^{2}\,,

then, with
\lambda=\frac{\lVert{v}\rVert}{\lVert{w}\rVert}
,

(G.8)

\lambda{f}\left(\frac{{v}}{\lambda}+{w}\right)\leq{f}({v}+{w})\,.
